OK, so the log contains a lot of info. The main issues:

    Parity FAIL: uMantle/wEq diverge before the plate solve (worst rel uMantle 1.2e+0, wEq 1.8e+0 > 0.001) - the mantle/forces field kernels disagree. At frame 5, the GPU is drastically diverging from the CPU (the mantle/force kernels don't match).

    Readback short on every layer: "readback short on layer plate: 0 of 2097152 bytes" — all layers have 0 bytes read back. And the cause: webgpu uncaptured: Source and destination are the same buffer in CopyBufferToBuffer(..., 2048, ..., 0, 296). This is a WebGPU validation error: copying a buffer to itself is invalid. In other words, somewhere in the code, copyBufferToBuffer is being called with the same source and destination buffer. As a result, the command buffer becomes invalid, submit fails, and the readback buffer stays empty → the render comparison ends up as "compared nothing" → black frames on screen? Actually, the user says "the black frames bug hasn't been fixed. Visible black frames are being generated at L6 step 1 and L5 step 5 (i.e., all the heavy ones)".

So the actual user-visible bug is black frames during heavy loads. Black frames = the GPU-side rendering pipeline fails at times when the simulation step is heavy (long frame), or the command buffer that draws the render layer is invalidated, or the readback buffer isn't copied, and… wait, "visible black frames" means the canvas is showing black frames.

Key WebGPU error: CopyBufferToBuffer with source==destination → validation error → the command buffer becomes invalid → the submit fails → the render pass that was in that command buffer doesn't execute → black frame.

The copy offsets in the errors are: CopyBufferToBuffer([Buffer], 2048, [Buffer], 0, 296), 2560 → 296, 4096 → 296. So it's copying 296 bytes from some offset into the same buffer at offset 0. This looks like an in-buffer memcpy (compaction/swap trick) that WebGPU doesn't allow: copying a buffer to itself is forbidden. 296 bytes… probably the size of a "ledger" or some state struct that gets moved within the same buffer to rotate between slots.

Therefore the fix is: don't do in-buffer CopyBufferToBuffer with the same buffer as both source and destination; use a temporary staging buffer or a kernel instead.

Found it — in GpuSim.batch (sim-gpu.js, Phase V), when n > 1, the code encodes the following:

JavaScript

enc.copyBufferToBuffer(S.buf.frameIn, (n - 1) * strideBytes, S.buf.frameIn, 0, GpuSim.FIN_FIELDS * 4);

This is a copy from a buffer to itself, which is exactly the same as the error in the log (CopyBufferToBuffer(..., 2048, ..., 0, 296) — 296 = 74 floats × 4). WebGPU/Dawn rejects copies from a buffer to itself, so the entire command buffer is invalidated, the submit fails, and the render tail that was added to the same encoder doesn't get drawn. Fix options:

    Replace the self-copy with a small staging buffer: allocate a 296-byte (or stride-sized) COPY_SRC|COPY_DST buffer once; in batch, copy frameIn[(n-1)*stride] → staging, then staging → frameIn[0]. Two copies between distinct buffers are legal. Wait — is copying from frameIn to staging and then from staging to frameIn[0] within the same encoder legal? The copy regions: src range [(n-1)*stride, (n-1)*stride+296), dst1 range [0, 296) of staging; second copy src staging [0,296), dst frameIn [0, 296). The spec allows multiple copies as long as copies don't overlap… The actual rule: "a copyBufferToBuffer must not overlap with a previously encoded copy" — the rule is that the source range and destination range of a copy must not overlap with the source or destination range of another copy within the same encoder, and if they do overlap, the behavior is undefined (or a validation error in Dawn). The two ranges here: copy1: frameIn[(n-1)*stride .. +296) → staging[0..296). copy2: staging[0..296) → frameIn0..296). [blocked]

Do copy1's src (frameIn high region) and copy2's dst (frameIn 0,296)) overlap? No (n>1, stride ≥ 296, so the high region starts at ≥ stride ≥ 320 > 296… wait, stride=512 on hardware, and (n-1)*stride ≥ 512 > 296, so no overlap). copy1's dst (staging) and copy2's src (staging) — the same buffer range is dst of one and src of the other. The spec says: "For copies, each copy's source and destination ranges must not overlap with any other copy's source or destination range"… Hmm, actually the exact WebGPU spec validation for copyBufferToBuffer: [blocked]

From the spec (GPUCommandEncoder / copies):

    A GPUCommandEncoder has a copy list… each copy is {source, destination}. Validation:

        if source is a buffer and destination is a buffer, then:
            source and destination must be different buffers?

Hmm, wait, I need to check the actual spec. Let me recall the WebGPU spec §"copy validation":

copy = {    source: GPUBuffer or GPUImageCopyTexture,    destination: ...}

The validation condition (from the spec):

        If source is a GPUBuffer and destination is a GPUBuffer:
            source must not be the same as destination?

Hmm, I don't remember exactly. Dawn's error message "Source and destination are the same buffer" strongly suggests that the spec forbids it. Let me check the actual WebGPU spec text… From webgpu/spec:

- If source is a `GPUBuffer` and `destination` is a `GPUBuffer`:  - ...  - The buffer copy must not be from a buffer to the same buffer

Hmm, I do think there's such a condition. The actual exact spec text (from my memory of the "copies" section of the WebGPU spec):

    For each copy in the encoder's copy list:

        if both source and destination are GPUBuffer:
            source and destination are different buffers
            source.usage includes COPY_SRC
            destination.usage includes COPY_DST
            ...

Yes, I'm fairly confident the spec forbids a buffer-to-itself copy ("source and destination must be different buffers" — the "Source and destination are the same buffer" validation error). OK.

So fix: use a small staging buffer (created once per session, added to the S.buf or S.stage set and released by release()).

    Alternative: eliminate the need for the copy. Why is the tail copy needed in the first place? Because diagFrame (and probably the fPlates readers) read from block 0 of frameIn. A cleaner fix: make diagFrame not depend on block 0 — but diagFrame is a diagnostic-only submit that reads the current buffers; the frameIn block 0 values (dt, alpha, plateCount, Tm, plumes…) describe the latest frame. After a batch, block 0 is the first frame's values. The only field that can change mid-batch is plateCount (plate splits) — t/Tm/plumes differ by only dt*i (small). The comment says the invariant is needed for fPlates().

Hmm, but actually — a simpler and cleaner alternative: instead of a tail self-copy, have the batch write the latest frame's scalars to block 0 via a different mechanism… The device queue's writeBuffer is a separate operation; we can just call device.queue.writeBuffer(S.buf.frameIn, 0, lastBlock) after the batch (before or after submit — writeBuffer is a queue operation that is ordered after prior submits). But wait: ordering. The batch submits an encoder that includes a dispatch reading frameIn blocks 0..n-1 and a tail copy. If instead we do:
js

GpuSim.batch: ... submit the encoder (no tail copy)device.queue.writeBuffer(S.buf.frameIn, 0, lastBlock296)

writeBuffer happens after the encoder's dispatch completes (queue operations are ordered). This preserves the invariant "block 0 holds the latest frame's scalars" for subsequent reads (diagFrame, or the next uploadFrame which overwrites block 0 entirely anyway). And it avoids the extra in-encoder copy entirely!

But a subtlety: frameBlocks computes blocks into S.finBlocks (a persistent Float32Array). The last block is at S.finBlocks[(n-1)finStride .. +74]. We can writeBuffer directly from that subarray: S.device.queue.writeBuffer(S.buf.frameIn, 0, S.finBlocks.subarray((n-1)*l.finStride, (n-1)*l.finStride + GpuSim.FIN_FIELDS)).

Hmm wait, but there's a comment about SwiftShader: "Subarray, not dataOffset: Chrome/SwiftShader rejects nonzero dataOffset" — so we use subarray, which is fine.

But note: writeBuffer on the queue while the just-submitted encoder is still in flight — that's legal (queue operations are serialized; writeBuffer waits for prior work that touches that buffer? Actually, per spec, writeBuffer copies are ordered after prior commands in the queue; the driver handles the sync). Yes, queue.writeBuffer is queued after the submit; it won't race.

But wait — a subtlety about the ordering of subsequent batch/frame operations: after batch() returns (having submitted), the next operation (another batch or frame) will uploadFrame/frameBlocks → writeBuffer(S.buf.frameIn, 0, ...) — these queue operations are ordered after the tail writeBuffer. No problem.

And a diagFrame submit after the batch: the encoder's dispatch reads block 0 — it's submitted after the tail writeBuffer is queued, so it reads the updated values. No problem.

Actually wait — which is better for the test: gpu-play.js test 8 asserts "the tail copy leaves block 0 holding the LAST frame's scalars" by checking GpuSim.S.buf.frameIn.bytes after GpuSim.batch(s, DT, 3, 2) on the stub. With queue.writeBuffer, the stub's queue.writeBuffer performs the copy immediately (synchronously) — so the assertion would still pass.

Hmm, but wait: is there any case where an encoder is built after the batch() call returns but needs to see the old block 0? Let me think… In play(), batch() is called, then state.t/frame advances. The next segment: batch() again → frameBlocks → writeBuffer(frameIn, 0, blocks) — overwrites everything. presentNow() → renderer.appendTo (no frameIn reads). roundTrip → download → diagFrame submit (reads block 0 — we want the latest). OK.

But hmm, another consideration: writeBuffer on frameIn while the batch's encoder is in flight — the encoder's dispatch has already read frameIn; the queued writeBuffer happens after. No problem.

However — one more important consideration: performance. writeBuffer of 296 bytes per batch: negligible (one memcpy into the driver). Much better than an extra copy pair or a stall.

Actually, wait, let me reconsider the alternative: just delete the tail copy entirely and change what reads block 0. What reads frameIn block 0 after a batch?

Let me check what the diag kernels and the rest of the engine read from frameIn block 0:

Confirmed: diagA/C/B binds frameIn, and uses fPlates() (FIN[6]) for the plate >= fPlates() sanity check (markBad → finite flag). Therefore, the invariant "block 0 of frameIn = the most recent frame's scalar values" is critical for the on-demand diagFrame after a batch. If the plate count increases mid-batch (split), the stale block 0 would undercount the plate count → diagA would markBad → the finite flag would be falsely triggered. So the tail-copy logic must be preserved, but it must not be a self-copy.

Now, the fix. Options:

Option A: queue.writeBuffer the final block into block 0 after the batch's submit.
js

if (n > 1) {    S.device.queue.writeBuffer(S.buf.frameIn, 0,        S.finBlocks.subarray((n - 1) * l.finStride, (n - 1) * l.finStride + GpuSim.FIN_FIELDS));}

Ordering: writeBuffer is queued after the encoder's submit → block 0 is updated after the batch's dispatches complete. Any subsequent encoder (diagFrame, etc.) that is submitted after this will see the updated value.

Wait — is this actually true? Queue operations are serialized in the order they are enqueued: submit(encoder) then writeBuffer. The writeBuffer's copy happens after the encoder's commands complete. And the next submit (e.g. diagFrame's encoder) happens after that. So, yes, it's ordered.

Cost: one small (296-byte) queue write per batch — same cost as the uploadFrame write that every frame() call already performs. Negligible.

Hmm, but one issue: the stub test. The stub's queue.writeBuffer performs the copy immediately at call time. The test asserts that block 0 of GpuSim.S.buf.frameIn.bytes equals finBlocks block 2 after GpuSim.batch(s, DT, 3, 2). In Option A, batch() calls writeBuffer at the end → the stub copies immediately → the assertion passes.

But wait — subtle point: in the stub, writeBuffer copies from S.finBlocks's subarray. But S.finBlocks is reused by subsequent frameBlocks calls (e.g. the next test GpuSim.batch(s, DT, GpuSim.FIN_MAX, -1)). In the stub the copy is synchronous, so by the time the next frameBlocks is called, the bytes in block 0 have already been captured. No problem.

On a real device, writeBuffer is a driver copy at queue time — the bytes are captured at the time of the call. S.finBlocks will be reused later, but the driver has already copied the bytes. No problem.

Option B: A dedicated small staging buffer + 2 copies within the same encoder. More complex, and the copy needs to be within the same submit as… no, actually it can't: if the copies are in the batch's encoder, they happen during the encoder's execution, i.e. after the dispatches — that's what we want, and it would be submitted together. Either works. Option A is simpler and also avoids the issue of copy ordering validation.

Hmm, wait — actually there's one more thing to consider about Option A. The invariant is "block 0 holds the most recent frame's scalar values," but which is more correct — is it actually correct in the first place? After a batch of frames 0..n-1, the most recent frame is n-1. Its scalar values (t, Tm, plumes, plateCount) describe frame n-1. A subsequent diagFrame diagnoses the device state after frame n-1 (all the other buffers hold frame n-1's results). So block 0 should be frame n-1's scalar values. That's exactly what the tail copy does. ✓.

But, hmm, one more consideration: after a batch, uploadFrame (from the next step/frame) writes its own scalar values into block 0 — but the next frame's dispatches also read block i's scalar values with a dynamic offset. No problem.

Now — what about the ordering between the batch's tsResolve and the writeBuffer? Irrelevant (different buffers).

Option C: Have the kernels that need it read the final block instead of block 0. diagFrame would need a dynamic offset — more invasive. Rejected.

Going with Option A.

Bench smooth:
js

function tick(now) {    ...    if (busy) return;    busy = true;    var renderTail = function (enc) {        try { planet.renderer.appendTo(enc, 'z'); } catch (e) {}    };    GpuSim.play(state, dt, n, null, { render: renderTail }).then(function () {        busy = false; done += n;        requestAnimationFrame(tick);    })...}

Note: the canvas is always drawn (appendTo) — the bench's canvas displays the planet. For n=1 (L6 steps=1): each tick calls play(state, dt, 1, null, {render}) → 1 segment (run=1) → batch(state, dt, 1, -1, tail) → tail is appended → submit.

But: the event round-trip! play() with n=1:
js

while (done < n) {   // n=1    if (done > 0 && hold && hold()) return done;    if (state.t - state.lastEvent >= P.eventCadence) {        presentNow();          // draws to canvas on a separate encoder + submits        await GpuSim.roundTrip(state, true, false, ...);   // ~28 ms at L6        ...    }    ... run=1 ...    GpuSim.batch(state, dt, 1, -1, takeRender());    ...}

When the event is due (every 10 frames = 1 Myr): play() first presentNow()s (canvas draw of the current sim state — no problem), then awaits the round-trip. The round-trip is: downloadEvents (pull the EVENT_READS buffer: mapAsync, await), Events.cycle (CPU), uploadEvents (writeBuffer + uploadFrame). Total ~28 ms (at L6). During this await, play's Promise is pending → busy remains true → the next rAF ticks return early (no drawing). The presented frame stays on screen (the compositor retains the last presented texture). So there's no black frame — just a ~28 ms freeze (the observed hitch: p95 105ms, hitches 24/250 at L6 steps=1).

Actually — let me focus. The priority is clear:

Bug 1 (certain, from the log): the self-copy in batch() invalidates every n>1 encoder. Fix: replace with a queue.writeBuffer of the last block (or a staging buffer). This alone fixes the smoke's 6 errors, the garbage iso numbers at n>1, and the artifact D1 divergence (after the fix, the GPU actually runs 20 frames and D1's layer diff becomes real data — D1 might still fail for a real reason, but at least the data will be valid).

Bug 2 (user-visible black frames): the canvas presents textures whose draw hasn't completed, under queue backlog at heavy configs. Fix: backpressure — don't enqueue a new frame's draw (and sim) while the previous frame's draw is still in flight. Cleanest, most general place: the play path awaits onSubmittedWorkDone at the segment boundary before enqueuing the next segment? Hmm, but that only bounds the backlog to 1 segment (up to 20 frames!). A 20-frame segment at L6 = 420 ms of GPU work → the next rAF draws immediately (same texture? a new one?) → present-before-complete → black again, just less frequent.

The real fix: each rAF's draw must be guaranteed to complete before it's presented. Since presentation happens at the next compositor refresh (~16.6 ms later), the draw must complete within that window — you can't guarantee that for a 21 ms sim… unless you draw from the last completed state: i.e. if the queue is more than ~1 frame deep, skip the canvas draw this rAF (the sim keeps running; the canvas keeps showing the last completed state — a freeze, no black).

So the fix is: present only when the queue has caught up — i.e. in the frame loop (app and bench), before submitting this rAF's sim+draw segment, check the GPU queue depth; if there's more than N (say 1) in-flight segments, skip the draw this rAF (sim only, no canvas texture acquired/drawn), or skip the whole segment (backpressure).

Which is better for the user's perception?

    Skip draw only (sim keeps running): the canvas freezes at the last completed state; the sim's backlog keeps growing (the GPU runs forever behind). The canvas only updates when the queue drains — which never happens for a persistently heavy config (21 ms of work every 16.6 ms → the queue never drains) → the canvas is frozen forever! Bad.
    Skip the whole segment (backpressure): the sim runs at the GPU's rate; the canvas updates every completion (~47 fps at L6×1); no backlog; no black.

Backpressure is the answer: gate the next segment's submit on the previous segment's completion (onSubmittedWorkDone). This makes the effective frame rate = min(requested rate, GPU rate), and the canvas always shows the latest completed state.

Now, where to implement it? The bench measures "the frame-gap distribution" — with backpressure, the gaps at heavy configs = true latency. I'd argue the bench should measure the true experience, so the backpressure belongs in the play path itself (GpuSim.play) — that way the app and bench get the same behavior.

But note: play() is an async function called from the rAF loop with a busy guard. If play() awaits onSubmittedWorkDone at the top of a segment (before building the encoder), the rAF tick that calls play returns a Promise that resolves after the GPU catches up → the busy guard prevents a new play until then → backpressure. For light configs, onSubmittedWorkDone resolves immediately (the work is already done) → no change.

Hmm, but a subtlety: onSubmittedWorkDone awaits all in-flight work, including this play call's previous segment (no problem) and the event round-trip (part of the play path, no problem), and other submits (diagnostic frame, tsCollect's map, a probe download — all of these are small). So: at the top of each segment in play(): await S.device.queue.onSubmittedWorkDone(); — hmm, wait, that would also await the work from the previous rAF's segment (good — that's the backpressure).

But wait — is that too aggressive? Consider the app at L5×1 (6.8 ms): rAF0 submits a segment (6.8 ms of GPU). rAF1 at +16.6 ms: play() awaits onSubmittedWorkDone → the work completed at 6.8 ms → immediate resolution → submit. No stall. ✓

L6×1 (21 ms): rAF0: submit (completes at 21). rAF1 at 16.6: play awaits → completes at 21 → resolves at 21 → submits a segment that completes at 42. rAF2 at 33: the busy guard? The .then fires at 21 → busy=false at 21 → rAF2 at 33: play awaits → completes at 42 → submits → completes at 63. So the rAF gaps: 21, 21, 21… ~47 fps, the canvas updates every ~21 ms with the latest state. No black. ✓ Myr/s = 0.1 Myr / 21 ms ≈ 4.7 (same as before). ✓

And the event round-trip inside play: after the round-trip (28 ms at L6), the next segment is submitted — with the backpressure await, that's after the previous GPU work… the round-trip's mapAsync already waits for the queue to drain (map waits for all prior work). So the await after the round-trip is cheap. ✓

Now — does the backpressure break the bench's "iso" measurement? iso doesn't use play (it uses batch + onSubmittedWorkDone directly) — unchanged (once the self-copy is fixed, the numbers become real).

Does the backpressure break the app's Step button (single step)? GpuSim.step → frame() — no backpressure added (a single-frame step doesn't back the queue up… well, 100 rapid clicks could, but the busy guard serializes them; each step is one encoder; at 6.8 ms the queue depth is bounded by click rate. No problem, leave it).

What about GpuSim.raster (boot)? One encoder. No problem.

So the changes:

    sim-gpu.js batch(): replace the self-copy with a queue.writeBuffer of the last block (after the submit? before?). Ordering: it has to happen after the encoder's dispatch (which reads all n blocks — no wait, the encoder's dispatch reads blocks 0..n-1; the writeBuffer to block 0 must not clobber block 0 while the encoder is still reading it! The encoder reads block 0 in frame 0's dispatch, and the last kernel (forces/reduceB) reads… do the last kernels read frameIn? forces (plates kernel) reads fPlates/fDT? Probably. So the writeBuffer to block 0 must be queued after the encoder → queue ordering guarantees that: the submit is enqueued first, then the writeBuffer → the writeBuffer's copy happens after the encoder's commands complete. ✓ So: inside batch(), after S.device.queue.submit([enc.finish()]), if n>1: S.device.queue.writeBuffer(S.buf.frameIn, 0, lastBlockSubarray).

Wait, hmm, but the tsResolve copy too… irrelevant.

But wait — one more check: S.finBlocks — the persistent array the blocks were written to. After the submit, we writeBuffer from S.finBlocks.subarray((n-1)*finStride, (n-1)*finStride+74). The driver copies the bytes immediately at queue time (before the next frameBlocks overwrites them). ✓ (writeBuffer copies from the JS memory at call time.)

Also GpuSim.frameBlocks sets S.finBlocks.fill(0, 0, l.finStride * n) then fills n blocks. The subarray of the last block has exactly 74 floats. ✓*

Edge: n=1 → no copy needed (block 0 is the only block — the invariant holds). ✓

Also, the comment in batch() must be updated to explain the writeBuffer (and why not an in-encoder self-copy — Dawn forbids a buffer-to-itself copy, and invalidates the entire encoder: sim+draw discarded, the bench iso for n>1 measures a discarded submit, the smoke's "D1 divergence" was an artifact).

    The stub (tests/gpu-stub.js): make the stub strict like Dawn: copyBufferToBuffer with src===dst → mark the encoder invalid (throw at finish()/submit, or record an error). This is the pitfall: "the stub validates nothing." The stub should at least catch a buffer-to-itself copy. Also maybe validate usage bits (COPY_SRC on src, COPY_DST on dst)? Keep it minimal but meaningful: a same-buffer copy throws an Error at submit/finish (like Dawn's invalid buffer). Also queue.submit with an invalid encoder → throw. And crucially, the stub's copyBufferToBuffer currently performs the copy immediately at encode time — with a strict invalidation, a throw at encode time (Dawn reports at encode; the error surfaces at finish as an uncaptured error). For test simplicity: throw at the call, or mark invalid and throw at finish(). Let me throw a clear Error at encode time (message "stub: copyBufferToBuffer from a buffer to itself is rejected (Dawn: source and destination are the same buffer)"). Hmm — but throwing at encode time changes the semantics: Dawn doesn't throw at encode; it records a validation error, the encoder becomes invalid, finish() returns an invalid buffer, and submit reports. For the test, throwing at encode time is the simplest and most meaningful.

But wait — the stub is also used by tests that exercise the round-trip: enc.copyBufferToBuffer(S.buf[name], 0, stage[name], 0, size) — different buffers, no problem.

    tests/gpu-play.js: the test 8 assertion "the tail copy leaves block 0 holding the LAST frame's scalars" — with the writeBuffer approach, the stub's queue.writeBuffer copies immediately → the assertion still holds (it checks buf.frameIn.bytes). ✓ No change needed. But I should add a test that pins the no-self-copy invariant: e.g. assert that the stub device observed no invalidation… or more directly: the strict stub itself is the pin — if batch() regresses to a self-copy, the stub throws and the test fails.     Black-frame backpressure: in GpuSim.play — where exactly? Let me design:

js

Add backpressure at the top of each segment: before building the batch's encoder, await S.device.queue.onSubmittedWorkDone();? Hmm wait — but the round-trip already drains the queue (mapAsync waits). But wait — there's a subtlety about the draw: the tail's draw is in the same encoder as the segment's sim. If we await full completion before building the encoder, then at submit time the queue is empty → the draw is the only work → completes within the GPU frame time (the sim's time! e.g. 21 ms at L6×1) → the present at the next refresh (16.6 ms) is still before completion for a 21 ms config!!! Ugh. So backpressure alone doesn't guarantee no black: even with a drained queue, a 21 ms sim+draw encoder submitted at t=0 completes at t=21, and the compositor presents the acquired texture at t≈16.6 (the next refresh) → black again, on every frame!

Wait — is that right? When does the compositor present the canvas's current texture? At each compositor frame (~every 16.6 ms). The texture becomes the "latest" when the write is submitted (t=0). The compositor presents the latest at the next refresh (t=16.6) — before the write completes (t=21) → presents black.

Let me look at the actual Chrome behavior for WebGPU canvases. From my knowledge of Chrome's implementation (WebGPUCanvasContext):

    Each compositor frame, the browser process asks the GPU process for the canvas's "latest texture".
    If a texture has been submitted (write submitted), it's returned; the GPU process presents it with a "present fence"…

Honestly, the exact mechanism is: Chrome presents the latest submitted texture at the next compositor frame. If the work that writes it isn't complete, ANGLE's swapchain… the texture is backed by a D3D12 texture; presentation via D3DSharedImage; Chrome tracks a per-texture GPU fence; if the fence isn't signaled, the compositor… I recall it waits a bit and then presents whatever is in the texture (i.e. black for an undrawn texture) — or it just presents and the shared image shows incomplete content.

The project's own empirical note ("getCurrentTexture after a yield presents black: L6 flash per cadence, L7 blank until pause") confirms: black happens when the texture is acquired/presented while work is pending. "L6 flash per cadence" = a black flash on each event cadence — that was the bug presentNow() fixed by drawing on a standalone encoder before the await. After the fix (a draw inside the batch encoder), at L7 the draw is still behind the sim in the same encoder — the sim is 100 ms+ per frame… and the user still sees black at heavy configs ("black frames bug not solved").

Actually — wait. We can verify the black frame mechanism headlessly by measuring rather than looking: instrument the canvas! After each rAF, check whether the presented texture was actually drawn… that's not easy to observe.

A better approach for the black frame: think carefully about what Chrome does, and design a fix that is correct under any presentation semantics:

Invariant we want to guarantee: the render pass that draws the canvas's current texture must complete before that texture is presented.

Given that presentation happens at each compositor refresh (~16.7ms), the only robust guarantee is: at the moment the compositor is about to present, the queue is either empty or contains only work that completes quickly… we can't control the compositor.

The practical standard approach (used in every WebGPU game/demo): don't queue more frames than the display can consume — i.e., gate each frame's submission on the previous frame's completion (a 1-deep pipeline). That is, at the start of each rAF's play() segment, await onSubmittedWorkDone() (or equivalent) before submitting a new segment. Then:

    The draw of frame k is the only work in the queue at submit time.
    It completes within that segment's GPU time T.
    If T < 16.7ms: the draw completes well before the next refresh → the presented texture is fully drawn. No black.
    If T > 16.7ms (a heavy config): the draw completes after the next refresh. Chrome presents… hmm, the same problem.

Ugh. So even a 1-deep pipeline doesn't guarantee no black for heavy configs. Unless Chrome waits for the fence.

So let's nail down what Chrome actually does. Let me search for the actual behavior: "WebGPU canvas presents before render pass completes black" / WebGPU spec canvas presentation semantics.

Actually — wait. Let me reconsider. Let me reread the WebGPU spec on canvas presentation:

From the spec (§3.2.4 "Presentation"):

    "When the user agent determines that a canvas element should be presented, it performs the following steps: … if the context's latest texture is not null: present the latest texture…"

And the update of "latest texture" (§3.2.3):

    "The context's latest texture is updated when a command buffer that contains a render pass or copy that writes to the current texture is submitted."

Hmm, I really do think the spec updates the latest texture at submit time. And presentation just presents whatever the latest texture is — the compositor must handle the GPU fence: in Chromium, WebGPUCanvasContext tracks a "present fence" — it submits a "present" that waits for the write fence. If the write hasn't completed by the compositor deadline… I think Chromium's compositor waits for the fence with a timeout, and if it times out presents stale/undrawn content → black.

Actually, I recall the specific Chromium behavior: for WebGPU canvas, the GPU process creates a GpuCanvasVideoFrame with the texture and a fence. The compositor's display compositor waits for the fence on the frame. If the fence isn't signaled within the frame deadline, the compositor… I think it uses the frame with whatever content is available (for a swapchain texture this can be black) — or skips (reuses the previous frame). I'm not 100% sure.

Empirical evidence from this project (user's report + old comments):

    "getCurrentTexture after a yield presents black: L6 flash per cadence, L7 blank until pause" — old bug: draw submitted after an await → black.
    User's current report: black frames on heavy configs (L6×1, L5×5).

Both are consistent with "a presented texture whose write didn't complete in time → black."

So the robust fix for heavy configs: don't submit the canvas draw for frame k until the previous frame's work has completed, and if this frame's total GPU work exceeds the frame budget, delay the draw… no, that still can't beat the 16.7ms for a 21ms frame.

The truly robust alternative: decouple sim and draw: the canvas always draws the last completed sim state. That is:

    Sim segments get queued freely (backpressure via onSubmittedWorkDone so the queue depth stays at 1 segment).
    The draw is a separate, tiny render pass (one triangle, ~0.1ms on GPU) submitted only when the queue is (nearly) drained.

Concretely, per rAF:

    If the queue has in-flight work beyond a small threshold → skip the draw this rAF (the canvas keeps showing the last presented frame — no black, a slight freeze).
    Otherwise (queue drained, or only our own draw pending): submit the sim segment + draw.

With 1-deep backpressure on the sim: the sim segment for frame k is submitted only after frame k-1's segment completes. At submit time, the queue contains only segment k (21ms of sim + draw). The next refresh is ~16.7ms away → the draw (queued after the sim) completes at ~21ms → the refresh at 16.7ms presents a texture whose draw is still in flight → black again at L6×1!

So for a config heavier than the refresh rate, we can't show frame k at refresh k+1. The choices are: (a) show the last completed frame (freeze) — no black; (b) show a black flash.

The only way to have no black and keep updating: submit the draw from the previous frame's state in the same encoder as that previous frame's sim — i.e., the draw is always the last op of the sim encoder (the current design!). Then, the texture presented at refresh R contains the draw that completed at the end of encoder E. The problem: if encoder E takes longer than the remaining time until refresh R, black.

Wait, no — hold on. If the draw is the last op of encoder E, and E completes at time t_E, then the texture is fully drawn at t_E. The compositor presents it at the first refresh after t_E… if the compositor waits for the fence (which it should, since it's presenting that texture), then presentation happens after t_E → no black, just a delay! The frame rate drops to 1/T (47fps for 21ms) — exactly what the user wants (smooth-ish, no black).

So, is the current design (draw inside the sim encoder) already (a)-ish? It depends on whether the compositor waits for the fence or presents at refresh time regardless. If it waits for the fence: no black even for heavy configs (just lower fps). If it presents at refresh time: black for heavy configs.

The user sees black on heavy configs → on Chrome 151/D3D12, the compositor does not wait long enough (or presents undrawn content at the refresh deadline).

Hmm, OK, wait, wait. Let me question my assumption that the queue backlog is even relevant in the first place. Let me reconsider: with the self-copy bug in place, what does the bench's canvas actually show at L5×5?

L5×5: play(n=5) → 1 segment of 5 frames (event cadence 1.0 Myr = 10 frames, so 5 fits) → batch(5) with the self-copy → invalid encoder → nothing runs → the canvas is not drawn. play() resolves immediately (submit fails, promise resolves). busy=false. Next rAF 16.7ms later: same thing.

Every 10 "frames" (state.t advances 0.5 Myr per rAF!): when the event deadline is reached: play() → presentNow() → a valid encoder that draws the canvas (from the old sim state — the sim never advanced, so the boot state) → submit → the canvas is drawn (boot world). Then await the round trip (~28ms on real hw; L5: ~10ms) → the next rAF continues.

So the bench canvas at L5×5: the planet is drawn every ~10 rAFs (every ~167ms), in between… the last presented texture persists (no new write submitted → the latest texture is unchanged). If "the latest texture is updated only at submit time of a writing command buffer" (spec), then the screen shows the boot world frozen with a redraw every 167ms. Not black!

But the user says black. So my mental model of Chrome's canvas presentation must be wrong somewhere. The actual behavior must be: a newly acquired current texture becomes the presented content at the next refresh, even if it's never written to (i.e., presentation tracks the acquired texture, not the last written one).

I was able to reproduce the user's log almost line by line on the sandbox rig (the 16 readback exceptions are a known artifact of the SwiftShader rig, unrelated). Next, I'll verify the exact canvas presentation semantics in the WebGPU spec. This is what's determining the black frame behavior:

The spec is clear. The key passage from the first search result (W3C WebGPU spec §21.2):

    [[drawingBuffer]], an image, initially a transparent black image...: The drawing buffer is the working-copy image data of the canvas. ... The drawing buffer outlives the [[currentTexture]] and contains the previously-rendered contents even after the canvas has been presented.

    [[currentTexture]]: The GPUTexture to draw into for the current frame. It exposes a writable view onto the underlying [[drawingBuffer]]. getCurrentTexture() populates this slot if null, then returns it. In the steady-state of a visible canvas, any changes to the drawing buffer made through the currentTexture get presented when updating the rendering of a WebGPU canvas. At or before that point, the texture is also destroyed and [[currentTexture]] is set to null, signalling that a new one is to be created by the next call to getCurrentTexture().

    [[lastPresentedImage]]: The image most recently presented for this canvas in "updating the rendering of a WebGPU canvas"

So the presentation flow ("updating the rendering of a WebGPU canvas"):

    Expire the current texture (set to null).
    Set lastPresentedImage to drawingBuffer (a reference, not a copy).
    On the next getCurrentTexture(): "Replace the drawing buffer" — "Replace the drawing buffer of this" — hmm, wait:

Looking at the steps shown for getCurrentTexture:

        If this.[[currentTexture]] is null:
            Replace the drawing buffer of this.
            Set this.[[currentTexture]] to the result of calling device.createTexture() ...

Hmm, wait — "Replace the drawing buffer" — what does that do? From my recollection of the spec: "Replace the drawing buffer" is: if the drawing buffer hasn't been read (no peek op), replace it with a new transparent black image?? Let me check... actually from the spec:

    "Replace the drawing buffer of context": If context.[[drawingBuffer]] is not being displayed... set it to a new transparent black image of the same size.

Hmm, I don't remember exactly. But the key point: after presentation, the drawing buffer is replaced (with transparent black!), and the new currentTexture wraps the new drawing buffer.

Now, the crucial part: "In the steady-state of a visible canvas, any changes to the drawing buffer made through the currentTexture get presented when updating the rendering of a WebGPU canvas."

So presentation happens on each "updating the rendering" step (compositor frame). At presentation, the currentTexture is expired. The presented content = the drawing buffer = what was last written through the currentTexture (or transparent black if never written).

Critical detail: "when the canvas is displayed or otherwise read... implementations must ensure that all previously submitted work (e.g queue submissions) have completed writing to it via [[currentTexture]]" — for READS. And at presentation time... the presented image is the drawing buffer — if a write to it is still in flight (submitted but not complete), the compositor must handle that — the spec says presentation takes "a reference, not a copy; the drawing buffer's contents can't change in-place after the current texture has expired" — so after expiring the currentTexture, no more writes to that drawing buffer are possible (the texture is destroyed → subsequent submissions writing to it fail validation / are discarded).

So the timeline of the heavy-config case in the current design:

    t=0: rAF0: getCurrentTexture() → currentTexture=T0 (wraps drawingBuffer D0). Encoder0: sim0 + draw0→T0. Submit at t=0.
    t=16.7: compositor "updating the rendering": present D0 (the drawing buffer). Expire T0 (destroy). Set lastPresentedImage=D0.
        If draw0 hasn't finished by t=16.7 (in the 21ms config it completes at t≈21): the presented D0 is... whatever the GPU wrote before t=16.7 — i.e. partial / undrawn. Undrawn = "initially a transparent black image" → with alphaMode 'opaque' → BLACK.
    t=16.7 (next rAF after expiry): getCurrentTexture() → currentTexture is null → Replace the drawing buffer (D1 = new transparent black) → T1 wraps D1. Encoder1: sim1 + draw1→T1. Submit.
    t=33.3: present D1 — draw1 (submitted at t≈16.7, completes ≈37.7) is in flight → D1 is partially/fully undrawn → BLACK.
    ...every frame is black until the queue backlog drains (which, for a persistently heavy config, never happens — each rAF adds 21ms and drains 16.7ms).

For a light config (6.8ms): draw0 completes at t≈7 < 16.7 → D0 is fully drawn at present time → good. ✓

This fully explains the user's observation: black frames on any heavy config, no black on light ones. And it explains why the old "L7 blank until pause" / "L6 flash per cadence" bugs were manifestations of the same thing: any time a texture is presented before its draw has completed, black.

Furthermore, this also explains the user's "L6 step 1 and L5 step 5": any config where a per-rAF segment's GPU time (sim + draw) exceeds the display interval. L6×1: 21ms > 16.7 → black every frame. L5×5: 5×6.8≈34ms > 16.7 → black. L5×20, L6×5, L6×20: worse. L5×1: 6.8 < 16.7 → OK.

Now — what's the correct fix? The presented content is the drawing buffer D_n, and it gets replaced (→ transparent black) every time a new currentTexture is created (every frame after expiry), so the only way to avoid black is: at the moment the compositor presents (the next refresh), the drawing buffer's draw must be complete.

Options:

Option 1: Don't create a new currentTexture until the previous frame's work is done. I.e. in the frame loop: only call getCurrentTexture() (via appendTo) when the queue has drained (onSubmittedWorkDone resolved). For heavy configs: the rAF ticks keep firing, but the draw is skipped until the previous encoder completes. Timeline for L6×1 (21ms/encoder):

    t=0: queue empty → create T0 (D0), submit encoder0 (sim0+draw0), completes at t=21.
    t=16.7: compositor presents D0 — draw0 completes at t=21 > 16.7 → still black at the first frame!!

Damn. Even with Option 1, the first frame after a heavy encoder's submit is black. Because a 21ms draw can't possibly complete within the 16.7ms present window.

Hmm wait — no, no. Hold on. If we gate "create new texture" on drain, then the sequence is:

    t=0: submit E0 (draw→T0).
    t=16.7: present D0 (in flight → black). T0 expires.
    rAF at t≈16.7: queue not drained (E0 completes at 21) → skip draw (no new texture).
    t=33.3: compositor presents... what? lastPresentedImage = D0 (already presented). The currentTexture is null (never recreated) → "updating the rendering" — hmm, what does presentation do when currentTexture is null? It just re-presents lastPresentedImage (D0) — D0's contents: draw0 completed at t=21 < t=33.3 → D0 now contains the full frame! → good frame at t=33.3!
    rAF at t≈33.3: queue drained (E0 done at 21) → create T1 (D1), submit E1 (sim1+draw1), completes at 54.
    t=50: present D1 (in flight → black at t=50)
    t=66.7: re-present D1 (draw1 done at 54) → good.
    ...

So the pattern is: good, black, good, black... at 50% duty?? Still bad — still visible black frames!

Ugh. Hmm. Wait — is that right? Does the compositor re-present the same image twice? "updating the rendering of a WebGPU canvas" happens on every compositor frame. lastPresentedImage=D0 is presented on both the t=16.7 and t=33.3 refreshes. At t=16.7, D0 is undrawn (black). At t=33.3, D0 is drawn (good). So yes: alternating black/good = flicker. No good.

Option 2: Present only fully-drawn content — i.e. delay the texture creation+submit so the draw completes before the next refresh. For a 21ms frame, impossible (21 > 16.7). So heavy configs can never update at 60Hz without black. The fundamental constraint: if the per-frame work is T > refresh interval, the canvas can only update at ≤ 1/T Hz.

So the real fix for heavy configs: update the canvas at the sim's completion rate, not the display rate — i.e. a single draw per completion of a segment, and crucially, don't start the next segment's draw until the previous present is safe...

Hmm, but the "present at next refresh" is automatic and uncontrollable. The only lever we have: when each frame's drawing buffer (D_n) is created and when its draw is submitted.

The black frame occurs when: at refresh time, the current D_n is undrawn. D_n is created at t_n (rAF that creates it) and its draw completes at t_n + T (roughly, if the queue is empty). Refreshes happen at 16.7ms boundaries. Black occurs when a refresh falls between t_n and t_n+T.

To avoid black entirely: choose t_n so that no refresh falls in (t_n, t_n + T)... for T > 16.7, impossible (T spans at least one refresh boundary).

Conclusion: for T > refresh interval, it's impossible to avoid black with a "draw this frame now" model. The presented image must be one whose draw already completed. So the architecture must be:

Option 3: Two-phase (draw the previous frame's state):

    Maintain a persistent offscreen "world texture" (our own, e.g. 1024×512).
    Each rAF (or on segment completion): submit [sim segment k + draw of state-after-k → worldTexture].
    The canvas presentation is a cheap blit: worldTexture → canvas currentTexture (a tiny ~0.1ms render pass).
    The blit is submitted only when the queue has drained (or at least when the worldTexture's latest draw has completed).

Presentation timeline for L6×1 (T=21):

    t=0: queue empty → blit current worldTexture (state -1) → T0 (D0), submit (0.1ms, completes at t≈0.2). Also submit segment0 (sim0 + draw state0 → worldTexture), completes at t≈21.
        Hmm wait, but then the blit of frame k shows state k-1 — one frame of lag. For slow-simulating geology, imperceptible.
    t=16.7: present D0 — fully drawn (0.2ms) → good (state -1).
    rAF t=16.7: queue not drained (segment0 in flight until 21) → skip blit (no new texture) — and don't submit segment1 either? If we submit segment1 (which queues after segment0, completes ≈42), the queue depth stays at 2. That's fine for the sim (no canvas involvement).
    t=33.3: present — no new texture created → re-present D0 (good, still state -1)...

Hmm wait, but then the canvas updates... when? When the queue drains:

    t=33.3 rAF: segment0 done at 21 but segment1 (submitted at 16.7) in flight until 42 → still not drained → skip.
    Every rAF keeps submitting a segment: the queue depth grows: segment k submitted at t=16.7k, completes at 21(k+1)+... depth grows without bound again. The canvas shows state -1 forever (blits only happen when drained, which never happens). No good!

So backpressure is needed on the sim too: submit segment k+1 only after segment k completes. Then:

    t=0: blit(state -1)→T0 submit; submit segment0 (done at 21).
    t=16.7: present D0 (good, state -1). rAF: not drained → no segment1, no blit.
    t=33.3: present re-D0 (good, state -1). rAF: drained (21 < 33.3) → blit(state 0)→T1 (created at 33.3, blit done ≈33.5); submit segment1 (done ≈54.5).
    t=50: present D1 — blit completed at 33.5 < 50 → good (state 0).
    t=66.7: present re-D1 (good). rAF: drained (54.5) → blit(state 1)→T2, submit segment2 (done ≈75.5).
    t=83.3: present D2 → good (state 1).
    ...

Canvas updates every ~33.3ms (2 frames) showing state that's 1 segment old: effectively 30fps of updates at ~17ms lag, zero black frames, sim runs continuously at 47fps (each 21ms segment).

For light config (T=6.8): work-done at t≈6.8 → next segment+blit at 6.8 (blit done 6.9 < present 16.7) → 60fps (or faster: segments complete at 6.8ms intervals, canvas updates every 6.8ms, but presentation refreshes at 16.7 — the compositor re-presents the latest D_n each refresh; new D_n created every 6.8ms... hmm, texture churn: 3 D_n's per refresh; the compositor presents the latest at each refresh → smooth 60fps showing the freshest. For heavy configs, the blit happens at the rAF right after drain (up to 16.7ms late → blit completes 16.7+0.1ms after segment completion → if the segment completed just before a refresh, the next refresh might catch it... let me check: segment k done at t=20.9; rAF at t=33.3 acquires D_n, blit done at 33.4; refresh at t=33.3?? Hmm, hmm. Let me step back and think about the simplest thing that correctly fixes the user's issue:

The user's issue: visible black frames on heavy configs (L6×1, L5×5, "so any heavy").

The root cause (established): the canvas's drawing buffer is created fresh (transparent black) every time we getCurrentTexture(), and the draw (which is queued behind a heavy sim in the same encoder) completes after the next compositor refresh → the refresh presents the undrawn (black) buffer.

Minimal robust fix: Never let the canvas's currentTexture be created/drawn in the same encoder behind heavy work. Instead: (a) The sim's encoder draws the world into our own persistent world texture (no canvas involvement). (b) The canvas presentation is a standalone tiny blit of world→canvas, submitted only when the queue has drained (guaranteeing the blit — a tiny pass — completes within the frame).

The drain-gated blit at rAF granularity gives: light config → 60fps (every rAF: drain → blit); heavy config → canvas updates every 2 rAFs (when the drain check passes), showing a state 1-2 segments old. Zero black under any config. Sim throughput unchanged (segments still queued per rAF; the queue depth grows as before, but... wait — if the sim's queue depth grows unboundedly, the "drain" check never passes for a persistently heavy config!! Same deadlock as before!).

Ugh. Right. So the sim also needs backpressure: submit segment k+1 only after segment k has completed (a 1-deep sim pipeline). Then for heavy configs: the sim runs at exactly the GPU's rate (47fps at 21ms/step), the queue is always ≤1 segment, the drain check passes every rAF after a completion, the canvas updates every ~2 rAFs (30fps) or 47fps with work-done-driven submission. For light configs: 1-deep is a no-op (the work is done before the next rAF) → 60fps, no change.

Myr/s numbers: L6×1: 0.1 Myr per 21ms = 4.76 (was 5.0 — the enqueued rate; now the achieved rate — more honest, roughly the same). Hmm wait — actually, hold on. With the 1-deep sim pipeline, does the bench's "smooth" frame gap measurement still make sense? The bench measures the rAF gap (the tick's now-last). If play() awaits the previous segment's completion before submitting the next one, the tick's gap = previous segment's remaining time → the gap for heavy configs ≈ segment time (21ms at L6×1, ~34+28/5... for L5×5: segment 5 frames ≈ 34ms + the event round-trip every 10 frames ≈ 28.5/2... roughly 48ms average?). Option 6 is elegant: the canvas texture is always drawn by the first op in its encoder → guaranteed complete long before its present. Hmm wait, but "draw old state first" — the draw reads the buffers (colF, cellF, etc.) as they are at the start of the encoder = state k-1 (the output of sim k-1). Then sim k mutates them. In a single encoder, ops execute in order (WebGPU encoders execute commands in order; within a compute pass there are barriers between passes). The draw pass reads the buffers; the sim passes write them. The draw is before the sim → reads the old values. ✓ Legal (no read-after-write hazard, the order is explicit).

The backpressure changes the bench numbers materially (L5×5 Myr/s 80.1 → ~15). Which is "right"? The bench measures "the full play path under rAF, reporting the frame gap distribution" + "Myr/s = simulated time per wall second." The 80 Myr/s number describes the work the GPU is queued to do, not what's happening. An honest bench reports the achieved rate. And crucially: an unbounded queue backlog is a memory/latency bomb: at L6×20 (a 420ms segment), 10 minutes of play = 3000 segments = 2 hours of GPU work queued. The mapAsync on the next event round-trip stalls for hours. The app freezes at the next event cycle. That's broken. The backpressure is needed. One more design question about the fix — the draw-first ordering and the bench's renderTail:
js

var renderTail = function (enc) {    try { planet.renderer.appendTo(enc, 'z'); } catch (e) {}};GpuSim.play(state, dt, n, null, { render: renderTail })

appendTo writes the uniform (a queue op — ordered before the encoder) + a render pass. As the first op in the encoder ✓.

But — a subtle issue: the draw reads the sim buffers as of the encoder's execution. With the backpressure, the encoder runs after the queue is empty → the buffers hold the last completed segment's state ✓. The canvas shows the last completed state — a 1-segment lag. ✓

And the app's "painted" logic: the tail runs in the first segment's encoder; the app checks painted && paintedView === viewVersion && paintedLayer === currentLayer after play resolves — the draw captured the view/layer at the tail's call time (the start of the rAF) — the same as today ✓.

One more: with the draw-first, the draw is before the diag frame (diagAt=last) — the diag reads the buffers after the sim ✓ no effect.

And the tsResolve: appended at the end ✓ no effect.

And — importantly — the tail's draw pass is before the zeroFrame pass (the sim's first pass, which writes the scan region). The draw doesn't read the scan ✓. The draw reads: lookup (static), gridI (static), colF, colI, cellF, cellI, edges — the sim's first pass (zeroFrame) writes scan, frameOut, the reduce regions — not the draw's reads ✓. No overlap, and the order is sequential anyway.

Now — the uniform: appendTo does device.queue.writeBuffer(this.uniform, 0, this.uniformBytes) — a queue op at the tail's call time (in the JS, before the encoder's submit) — the uniform is updated before the draw runs ✓.

Edge — the bench's presentNow case: play opens on a due event → presentNow() creates a standalone encoder [draw] → the backpressure await (top of the iteration) guarantees the queue is empty → the draw runs in ~0.3ms → no black ✓. Then the round-trip (mapAsync waits for the draw — 0.3ms — no problem).

Actually — a simpler, uniform rule: the canvas's currentTexture must only be acquired when the queue is empty. Enforce that in GpuRenderer itself! Add a drain wait to appendTo/draw:

    draw(layer): make it async? draw() is called synchronously in many places (the bench's buildPlanet: renderer.draw('z') without await; the smoke: renderer.draw(layers[l]) without await, then await readPixels(); the app's frame loop). Making draw() async (returning a promise) is safe (the callers ignore the promise; the readPixels await chains correctly since the copy happens after the draw's submit — the order: draw() submits the draw encoder (after the drain) → readPixels's copy encoder is queued after that ✓).

So making draw() async breaks the smoke's ordering. I need to keep the draw's submit synchronous (relative to the caller) — i.e. the drain wait must not delay the submit... but the drain is precisely what delays things.

GpuRenderer.prototype.present = function () {    var device = GpuSimRef.S.device;    var enc = device.createCommandEncoder();    var blit = enc.beginRenderPass({ colorAttachments: [{ view: this.context.getCurrentTexture().createView(), loadOp: 'clear', storeOp: 'store', clearValue: {r:0,g:0,b:0,a:1} }]});    blit.setPipeline(this.blitPipeline); blit.setBindGroup(0, this.blitGroup); blit.draw(3); blit.end();    device.queue.submit([enc.finish()]);};

New:
js

batch: function (state, dt, n, diagAt, tail) {    var S = GpuSim.S, l = S.l;    GpuSim.frameBlocks(state, dt, n);    var strideBytes = l.finStride * 4;    var enc = S.device.createCommandEncoder();    for (var i = 0; i < n; i++) { ... }    S.finOffset = 0;    GpuSim.tsResolve(S, enc);    // The visible frame's draw is appended here (tail), after the dispatches: the draw    // reads the sim buffers the way they are after frame n-1 ...     if (tail) tail(enc);    S.device.queue.submit([enc.finish()]);    // The single-frame invariant "block 0 holds the latest frame's scalars" (an on-demand    // diagFrame reads fPlates() from offset 0 after a mid-batch spawn) used to be a    // CopyBufferToBuffer from frameIn onto itself - WebGPU rejects a buffer copied to    // itself, invalidates the whole encoder and the submit runs nothing (the bench's    // n>1 iso measured a discarded submit, the smoke's D1 diff compared a GPU world that    // never advanced). The driver copies the bytes at queue time, so a writeBuffer of the    // last block, queued after the submit, keeps the invariant without an in-encoder copy.    if (n > 1) {        S.device.queue.writeBuffer(S.buf.frameIn, 0,            S.finBlocks.subarray((n - 1) * l.finStride, (n - 1) * l.finStride + GpuSim.FIN_FIELDS));    }},

I now have the full picture. Let me carefully design the final changes:
Design (final version)
Root causes

    Phase V self-copy: batch() encodes copyBufferToBuffer(frameIn, (n-1)*stride, frameIn, 0, 296) — copying a buffer into itself. WebGPU/Dawn rejects this → the entire command buffer is invalidated → the submit does nothing. Impact: (a) the sim never runs on any n>1 batch (the smoke "D1 divergence" is an artifact, the bench's n>1 iso numbers are garbage ≈ discarded submits), (b) on n>1 settings, the canvas draw (the tail of the same encoder) never runs.
    Black frames on heavy settings: the canvas's drawing buffer is replaced with a transparent black image every time getCurrentTexture() is called after presentation. The current code draws the canvas as the last op of a heavy sim encoder (or, in the self-copy case, not at all), so whenever a per-rAF segment's GPU work exceeds the display interval (L6×1: 21 ms, L5×5: ~34 ms, "so any heavy"), at the point the compositor presents, the drawing buffer is still undrawn → a visible black frame.
    Unbounded queue backlog: play() enqueues one segment per rAF without waiting for the GPU → on heavy settings the queue grows without bound (a mapAsync at the next event cadence stalls behind it, and the canvas falls further and further behind).*

Fixes

    GpuSim.batch: replace the self-copy with queue.writeBuffer(frameIn, 0, lastBlock) queued after the submit (the driver copies the bytes at queue time; queue ordering guarantees it runs after the encoder's dispatch). Keeps the "block 0 = latest frame's scalars" invariant that on-demand diagFrame needs.
    World texture + drain-gated blit (render-gpu.js):
        this.world: a persistent texture (RENDER_ATTACHMENT|TEXTURE_BINDING|COPY_SRC), canvas-sized. All layer draws (appendTo, redraw) target the world — never the canvas.
        present(): a tiny blit world→canvas currentTexture. Contract: only call when the device queue has drained (the caller guarantees it via queue.onSubmittedWorkDone().then(...)). The blit is ~0.2 ms, the queue is empty → it completes before the next refresh → zero black frames under any load.
        draw(layer) becomes redraw(layer) (standalone [world draw]) — callers that need the canvas also call present (drain-gated).
        readPixels is unchanged (copies the world).
    1-deep pipeline in GpuSim.play: before building each segment's encoder, await queue.onSubmittedWorkDone() (after the drag-gate check, around the round-trip block). Bounded queue (≤1 segment), honest frame gap / Myr/s, canvas lag is constant.
    App (js/ui.js): the frame loop blits via presentWhenDrained() (onSubmittedWorkDone → gpuRenderer.present()) instead of a standalone draw; standalone world redraws for paused / view-only / step frames + drain-gated present.
    Bench (bench.html): the smooth tick adds a drain-gated present after play; the boot's draw becomes redraw + drain-gated present.
    Stub (tests/gpu-stub.js): strict like Dawn — a same-buffer copyBufferToBuffer invalidates the encoder (throw on finish/submit), so a regression can never pass the node tests again.
    Tests: update gpu-readback.js (the app mode now also has a world texture; draw = redraw+present), gpu-play.js (add an explicit no-self-copy pin + keep the block-0 invariant), gui.js FakeRenderer (add present() tracking; the play assertion accounts for the blit being a drain-gated present).
    Docs: the 0.3-plan.md Phase V/VII notes, the findings-pitfalls-skills.md entry, the 0.3-7-prompt? No — that prompt is a session artifact; leave it. The bench subtext / note update is minimal.

A simpler, more robust rule: the frame loop only presents when it knows the world is up-to-date:

    mergedThisFrame: the tail (this rAF's play call) drew the world → present is safe (the blit is queued after the tail's encoder — both are in the same queue; the blit reads the world at execution time = after the tail ran ✓).
    non-merged gpu branch: the redraw is submitted now (the world is drawn at execution time) → present after the drain ✓ (the blit is queued after the redraw).
    rebuild: bootEngine's .then sets dirty=true → the next rAF's non-merged branch: redraw + drain-gated present ✓ (no stale present: the pre-rebuild presentWhenDrained's pending .then — the gpu.ready guard: false during the rebuild; after the rebuild completes, the pending .then (from before the rebuild) fires: gpu.ready is true again, gpuRenderer is the new one → it presents the new world (which may not yet have been drawn — the first redraw is this rAF's dirty branch)...

Ordering within one rAF: the frame(now) function: [play block] → [dirty block] → Perf... the pre-rebuild pending .then is a microtask — it can fire at any microtask boundary (e.g. between rAF frames, or even inside the current rAF if there's an await — the frame loop has no await; microtasks run after the current task (the rAF callback) completes). So the pending .then fires after the rAF callback ends → the blit is submitted after this rAF's redraw submit → the blit is queued after the redraw ✓ safe! Because JS is single-threaded: the redraw submit (a synchronous queue.submit inside the frame callback) happens before the microtask .then runs. ✓

But on the real device: with the backpressure await, when does the tail run relative to the rAF callback?

Timeline (real device, queue empty): rAF callback: play() called → segment 1: no due event → await onSubmittedWorkDone() — the queue is empty → the promise is already resolved → the await suspends until a microtask — the rest of play (the batch with the tail) runs in a microtask, after the current rAF callback returns... no wait — a microtask runs after the current task completes, before the next task. The rAF callback is a task; it calls play() (which returns a promise); the callback continues synchronously (the dirty block, Perf, requestAnimationFrame), then the callback returns; then the microtasks run: the continuation of play() → the drain resolves → the batch is built + the tail runs (painted=true, the world is drawn, the encoder is submitted).

So in the real device, the tail runs after the rAF callback — mergedThisFrame (read synchronously inside the callback) is always false → the app's "merged draw" optimization never fires → every rAF during play: the non-merged branch: redraw(layer) + drain-gated present → an extra standalone world redraw per rAF (a tiny pass, ~0.2 ms — cheap) + a present after the drain. So the batch() signature changes: it must support "build the encoder + dispatches synchronously, submit after the await". Refactor: extract the encoder construction from the submit:
js

// Build one segment's encoder (the n frames' dispatch graph + tail + ts resolve).// The caller submits it - play() awaits a queue drain in between so the sim pipeline// stays one segment deep.encodeBatch: function (state, dt, n, diagAt, tail) {    var S = GpuSim.S, l = S.l;    GpuSim.frameBlocks(state, dt, n);    var enc = S.device.createCommandEncoder();    for (var i = 0; i < n; i++) {        if (S.tsOn && i === 0) { S.tsActive = true; S.tsSlot = 0; }        S.finOffset = i * l.finStride * 4;        GpuSim.dispatchGraph(S, enc, state, diagAt === i, l);        if (S.tsOn && i === 0) S.tsActive = false;    }    S.finOffset = 0;    GpuSim.tsResolve(S, enc);    if (tail) tail(enc);    return enc;},batch: function (state, dt, n, diagAt, tail) {    var enc = GpuSim.encodeBatch(state, dt, n, diagAt, tail);    GpuSim.commitBatch(enc, n);    return enc;},// Submit a segment encoder, then restore the single-frame invariant (block 0 = the// latest frame's scalars) with a queue write - not an in-encoder copy: WebGPU rejects// a buffer copied onto itself and invalidates the whole encoder, so the old tail// copy discarded every n>1 batch (the sim never ran, the bench iso measured a// discarded submit, the smoke compared a GPU world that had never advanced).commitBatch: function (enc, n) {    var S = GpuSim.S, l = S.l;    S.device.queue.submit([enc.finish()]);    if (n > 1) {        S.device.queue.writeBuffer(S.buf.frameIn, 0,            S.finBlocks.subarray((n - 1) * l.finStride, (n - 1) * l.finStride + GpuSim.FIN_FIELDS));    }}

And play():

while (done < n) {    if (done > 0 && hold && hold()) return done;    if (state.t - state.lastEvent >= P.eventCadence) {        presentNow();        await GpuSim.roundTrip(state, true, false, GpuSim.Events, GpuSim.Checkpoint);        if (S.diagWanted) { want = true; S.diagWanted = false; }    }    var run = 0, tj = state.t;    while (...) {...}    if (run < 1) run = 1;    var diagHere = want || (opts && opts.diagLast && done + run === n);    // Build the segment's encoder now (the render tail runs synchronously, so the    // page's painted bookkeeping sees it in this rAF), then hold the submit until the    // queue has drained: one segment deep. An unbounded backlog made the heavy    // settings' canvas fall further behind every frame, and the round trip's    // readback stalled behind everything queued before it.    var enc = GpuSim.encodeBatch(state, dt, run, diagHere ? run - 1 : -1, takeRender());    want = false;    await S.device.queue.onSubmittedWorkDone();    GpuSim.commitBatch(enc, run);    for (var j = 0; j < run; j++) { state.t += dt; state.frame++; }    done += run;    if (state.ckptCap > 0 && state.t >= state.ckptDue) {        await GpuSim.roundTrip(state, false, true, GpuSim.Events, GpuSim.Checkpoint);        if (S.diagWanted) { want = true; S.diagWanted = false; }    }}return n;

Hmm — but: the tail's uniform writeBuffer (the view/layer) is at build time (rAF start). If a new play's build happens while the previous segment is still running — the uniform is set for the new segment; the new segment's world draw (queued after the drain) uses the new uniform ✓; the previous segment's world draw uses its own uniform (written before it) ✓ — the uniform is a single buffer shared by both — the writeBuffer ordering: the previous segment's draw runs first (it was submitted first), but the uniform write for the new segment was enqueued before the new segment's draw... the queue order: [prev segment encoder (world draw reads the uniform at execution time)] ... [new uniform writeBuffer] [new segment encoder]. The previous segment's world draw executes before the new uniform write is enqueued (the queue executes in order; the previous encoder was submitted earlier) → it reads the old uniform ✓. The new segment reads the new uniform ✓. No conflict (this is the same as today's uniform handling — no change).

The stub's strictness: make the stub's copyBufferToBuffer(src, ...) mark the encoder invalid if src === dst, and throw on finish() / submit:
js

createCommandEncoder: function () {    var invalid = null;    return {        copyBufferToBuffer: function (src, srcOff, dst, dstOff, size) {            // Dawn rejects a buffer copied onto itself and invalidates the whole            // encoder; a stub that lets it through cannot catch it (the Phase V tail            // copy shipped exactly that).            if (src === dst) { invalid = 'copyBufferToBuffer: source and destination are the same buffer'; return; }            new Uint8Array(dst.bytes).set(new Uint8Array(src.bytes, srcOff, size), dstOff);        },        beginComputePass: ...,        finish: function () { if (invalid) throw new Error('stub: encoder invalid: ' + invalid); return {}; }    };}

Hmm — queue.submit(buffers) — in the real Dawn, the error surfaces on submit; the finish() in the code returns an invalid buffer (no throw at encode time; the error is "uncaptured"). For the stub, a throw at finish() is enough to fail the test loudly. But — the code path: batch → enc.finish() throws → batch throws → play rejects → the app catches (the "GPU engine error" probe line) / the test fails. GpuRenderer.prototype.init = function (state, opts) {    ...    this.context.configure({ device: device, format: this.format, alphaMode: 'opaque' });    ... lookup buffer, uniform, pipeline ...    // The world texture: the engine's working image. Every layer draw (appendTo, redraw)    // lands here; the canvas only ever receives the blit (present). The spec replaces a    // presented canvas's drawing buffer with a fresh transparent-black one on the next    // getCurrentTexture, and the compositor presents at the next refresh whatever is in    // it then - so a pass queued behind a heavy segment finishes after the refresh and    // the frame presents black: every setting whose per-frame GPU work outruns the    // refresh used to flash (L6 1 step, L5 5 steps, "so any heavy"). The blit is a    // sub-millisecond pass the caller submits on a drained queue, so it always completes    // before its presentation.    var w = this.canvas.width, h = this.canvas.height;    this.world = device.createTexture({ size: [w, h], format: this.format, usage: 0x10 | 0x4 | 0x1 });    this.worldView = this.world.createView();    var blit = device.createShaderModule({ code: GpuRenderer.BLIT });    this.blitPipeline = device.createRenderPipeline({ ... });    this.blitGroup = device.createBindGroup({ ..., entries: [ {binding: 0, resource: device.createSampler(...)}, {binding: 1, resource: this.worldView} ] });    if (opts && opts.readback) this.initReadback(device);    return this;};GpuRenderer.prototype.initReadback = function (device) {    var w = this.canvas.width, h = this.canvas.height;    this.readRow = Math.ceil(w * 4 / 256) * 256;    this.readSize = this.readRow * h;    this.staging = device.createBuffer({ size: this.readSize, usage: 0x1 | 0x8 });    return this;};

// Append the layer draw to an encoder the caller owns and submits. It targets the world// texture, never the canvas: a canvas pass queued behind this segment's compute would// finish after the next compositor refresh and present black (see init).GpuRenderer.prototype.appendTo = function (enc, layer) {    var id = GpuRenderer.LAYERS[layer];    if (id === undefined) id = 0;    this.layerWord[0] = id;    var device = GpuSimRef.S.device;    device.queue.writeBuffer(this.uniform, 0, this.uniformBytes);    this.passInto(enc, this.worldView);};// A standalone world draw (the paused / view-only / rig path). The canvas is not touched.GpuRenderer.prototype.redraw = function (layer) {    var device = GpuSimRef.S.device;    var enc = device.createCommandEncoder();    this.appendTo(enc, layer);    device.queue.submit([enc.finish()]);};// The canvas blit: world -> current texture, one sub-millisecond pass. Contract: submit// on a drained queue (queue.onSubmittedWorkDone), or the presented frame can still be// in flight at the refresh - the black-frame bug this split exists to remove.GpuRenderer.prototype.present = function () {    var device = GpuSimRef.S.device;    var enc = device.createCommandEncoder();    this.passIntoCanvas(enc);    device.queue.submit([enc.finish()]);};

GpuRenderer.prototype.passIntoCanvas = function (enc) {    var blit = enc.beginRenderPass({ colorAttachments: [{        view: this.context.getCurrentTexture().createView(),        loadOp: 'clear', storeOp: 'store', clearValue: { r: 0, g: 0, b: 0, a: 1 }    }] });    blit.setPipeline(this.blitPipeline);    blit.setBindGroup(0, this.blitGroup);    blit.draw(3);    blit.end();};
